The role of participation in global world cities Maureen M. Donaghy INTRODUCTION Participation in organized groups is often difficult, sometimes boring, and always time con- suming. Voting can also be unappealing, particularly if none of the candidates represents an ideal choice. But politics cannot be transparent, responsive, or accountable to citizens without public participation. This is what we as scholars, activists, and advocates believe to be true, but research on public participation of various forms and in different contexts over the past few decades has sought to provide evidence as to when and how participation matters in public policy making, electoral politics, and neighborhood development. In the urban context these questions are critical as cities are the sites of protest, advocacy, community organizing, and electoral contestation. Scholars, along with government officials, practitioners, and activists, need answers to questions about participation to guide public policy making and institutional design in the modern democratic city. This brief chapter, then, examines what I hold as one of the most central questions for urban politics: When does citizen participation matter? As the chapter will discuss, questions of impact often center on who participates in political activities, why these individuals and groups choose to engage with the state, and how they ultimately make demands or enact change, from the neighborhood to the global level. Over the past several decades, the clash between capitalism and democracy across all these levels has become clearer along with questions of identity, inequality, and who decides the future direction of cities. Citizens come into conflict with developers and governments are meant to mediate claims to urban space. The extent to which citizens particularly those traditionally marginalized from politics can influence government decisions in the face of pressure to generate profits and public revenues often guides central debates in research on participation in urban politics. But in the last two decades scholars increasingly rely on the principles of the right to the city as envisioned by Henri Lefebvre in the 1960s to measure the extent to which cities reflect the desires of residents rather than a small segment of elites. Building on the work of Lefebvre, Purcell argues for a conception of democracy as a tendential project in which people continually increase their desire to manage their affairs themselves . In the urban context this means that people seek to manage the production of urban space on their own terms. In addressing when participation matters, then, the impact may be defined by the extent to which people, rather than governments or corporations, hold power in deciding the tangible and intangible characteristics of cities that shape their everyday lives. But what does this participation look like? In defining democratic norms scholars gen- erally argue that those affected by official decisions should have the right to participate in making them, or at least having their voices heard at some point in the policy-making process. Participation enables transparency in that public officials have to defend their positions, responsiveness in that those most affected must be involved in order to get the policies right, and accountability when officials must report results of policy implementation. But though Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. The role of participation in global world cities 287 we might now see participation as necessary in municipal governance this hasn t always been the case. The idea that citizens might be involved in planning only came about in the 1950s and 1960s when problems with top-down planning became apparent within slum clearance and urban renewal programs. Public officials at the time had not realized the problems these programs would cause to residents. In response, the 1960s War on Poverty programs required the maximum feasible participation of the poor in antipoverty programs. Today, almost all federally funded urban programs in the United States now have this requirement, formally institutionalizing the norm of citizen participation with the goal of incorporating the most vulnerable and often invisible individuals into decision making. But the form that citizen participation takes is certainly not static. Though participa- tion has become more institutionalized in global cities, in the past decade new forms of mobilization around issues of gentrification and displacement as well as race, migration, education, and public safety have renewed interest in research on what leads individuals to join collective movements, how these movements seek change, and what the outcomes of various claims-making strategies may be. As we move forward with research on participation, increasingly understanding when citizens influence the democratic process is key to innova- tions in institutional design and ensuring urban policies and programs respond to the needs of residents. The remainder of this chapter seeks to explore the threads of these arguments further, concluding with what we know about when citizen participation matters for determining the purpose and use of cities as well as directions for future research. WHAT IS PARTICIPATION? To understand when participation matters, of course we must first start with defining the vague independent variable of participation and how it has evolved over time. In global cities, participatory practices generally include: voting, running for office, engaging in open forums, taking part in street protests, debating on social media, contacting public officials, providing testimony, and writing letters, among numerous other possibilities. Dahl argued for the primacy of public contestation and participation as the bedrock of democratic polyarchy. Participation, in his view, was primarily defined by engagement with the electoral process through party formation, voting, and campaigning, and he argued that the opportunity for civic engagement was necessary to mediate conflicting interests in democracy, concepts that we mostly now take for granted. Verba and Nie then defined participation as behavior meant to influence the actions of government officials and policy-making decisions, broad- ening the concept from the electoral process to other types of lobbying and advocacy. Since then, other authors have found this definition too narrow, particularly outside of the United States context. For example, examining political events in five Western democracies, Barnes and Kaase , include all activities meant to directly or indirectly influence the political system, specifically incorporating protest and violent activities and not just elections or direct claims-making of the state. In the context of social movements, Sidney Tarrow framed participation in terms of contentious vs. cooperative activities, either meant to disrupt the status quo or work with public officials to achieve demands. More recently comparative urban scholars have included the means of influence, in which advocates seek power directly through formal institutions or Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. 2 Handbook of urban politics and policy mediated through efforts to influence voting and policy making from the outside . Eleonora Pasotti defines experiential tools embedded in cultural practices as repertoires of collective action, particularly in bringing attention to issues such as displace- ment and gentrification in cities worldwide. These types of performative activities often go hand in hand with voting and other traditional forms of protest to enable more voices to be heard, particularly from marginalized communities. Communities, themselves, also define what participation means to them. In the 1960s, Saul Alinsky urged urban residents to engage in community organizing, building on a community s power resources and assets to empower residents to bring about positive change. Since then, community organizations in the United States have largely turned away from protest towards more cooperative tactics, forming partnerships with public agencies to improve service deliv- ery and other conditions in neighborhoods. Community development corporations , which are neighborhood-based-non-profits, work with public and private entities to secure investments in low-income communities, and have had great success in generating and pre- serving affordable housing, health clinics, food banks, and other necessities. Working within these types of organizations may also be seen as a form of political participation in cities. In fact, Ostrom emphasized that communities have largely already developed ways of dealing with the challenges they face, and rather than imposing solutions from above the state should empower residents to further take charge. She developed the concept of co-production, in which citizens participate directly in the design and delivery of public services. Research by scholars such as Bovaird continues to emphasize the benefits of long-term relationships between public service users, community groups, and public sector bureaucrats through the process of co-production. Further, in the past two decades, research on participation has also shifted to understand the role of social media and new forms of participatory governance institutions as political partic- ipation. Social media serves as a platform for organizing in-person gatherings and events, but as Castells argues, it is also a mechanism for inciting participation through engaging with users emotions. Research continues to explore for whom social media influences par- ticipation and the impact of social media use on electoral choices, participation in advocacy campaigns, and other forms of individual and collective action. Continuing to calculate the balance between citizens and the state, the study of participation in governance arrangements has also evolved in the recent past. From public management literature, participation includes a broad range of governance activities, defined by Torfing . as the complex process through which a plurality of actors with diverging interests interact in order to formulate, promote and achieve common objectives by means of mobilizing, exchanging and deploying a range of ideas, rules and resources. Participation in governance processes is increasingly formalized in cities around the world. Literature on par- ticipatory governance institutions has proliferated over the last two decades, particularly since the installation of a participatory budgeting process in Porto Alegre, Brazil, which has served as a model for thousands of other cities. Scholars have sought to understand how par- ticipatory processes evolve, who participates, and how practices transfer to different contexts. Since the 1960s, our knowledge and understanding of what constitutes political partici- pation in cities has only grown as has our definition. The multitude of activities possible as forms of urban participation provides endless opportunities for research. But scholars must also be mindful to explicitly define participation in the urban context relevant to our places of study to clarify its meaning and impact for the population. If we want to know when citizen Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. The role of participation in global world cities 289 participation matters for shaping our cities, how we define participation must clearly move beyond electoral politics and traditional forms of protest to everyday forms of claims-making, cooperation, autonomous solutions, social networking, and shared governance. From the broadening definition of participation, we see the myriad ways that individuals and groups act, reinforcing the notion that there is no one set of actions that matters for shaping the lives of residents within cities. WHO PARTICIPATES? Though we see that citizen participation never takes just one form in any context, who par- ticipates should influence the voices that rise to the top of policy and decision making. For decades research and evidence as to who participates in politics in cities often focused on socioeconomic and demographic characteristics. From the 1960s questions of the relationship between income, education, and participation took priority. For example, Verba, Schlozman, and Brady found that lower-income and less educated citizens in the U.S. participated in politics less than those who were wealthier and more educated. Men (especially white men) have been more likely to participate in direct contact and collective action than women (Mansbridge 1983; Kittilson 2016). The elderly have higher levels of participation (Campbell 2003; Schlozman, Verba, and Brady 2012) as do people who have lived in their homes longer (Kang and Kwak 2003; Gay 2012) and own their homes . Wealthier citizens are also more likely to get involved in required participatory processes in the U.S. and there is evi- dence of voluntary participatory processes being dominated by middle- and upper-class urban residents in NYC , Minneapolis , and Los Angeles . All of these findings suggest a bias in conflict with the goal of enabling diverse voices in politics and policy making. Further, in recent challenges to spatial and resource distribution for housing in cities, researchers have found similar profiles for those who get involved in local politics. For example, Einstein, Palmer, and Glick compare individuals participating in planning and zoning board meetings on housing development to voter files in cities in Massachusetts, finding that people who are older, male, longtime residents, voters in local elections, and homeowners are significantly more likely to participate in these meetings. This group is also more likely than the general population to oppose new housing construction, leading to the conclusion that who participates in local politics has significant policy implications for development. From the studies above we see that who participates matters for shaping the city. And research on inclusivity in cities shows that neighborhood participation is key to providing voice to underrepresented groups and enhancing democracy (Berry, Portney, and Thomson 1993; Fung 2006; Michaels and de Graaf 2010; Stone and Stoker 2015). What we need is more research on how to ensure these groups do in fact get involved and that we design processes to meet diverse needs and interests in order to correct imbalances in power and representation. In terms of increasing diversity in who votes, research has shown mixed effects on specific interventions, such as early voting and initiatives to ease the burden of registration. Berinsky actually finds that these reforms are associated with an increase in socioeconomic ine- qualities in turnout. Burden and Neiheisel , however, finds that election day registration increases overall turnout, but early voting can decrease turnout without other reforms. Putnam Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. 2 Handbook of urban politics and policy argues that the erosion of social capital, particularly in urban areas, is behind the lack of civic engagement in town halls, rallies, organizations, unions, and religious organizations. So what does motivate urban residents to get involved in politics? Digging deeper, scholars have discovered further trends about why individuals choose to participate in political activ- ities. For example, economics and ideology often provide the motivation for mobilization around specific issues, such as financialization of housing and segregation of territory. Anger is also a powerful emotion associated with an increase in electoral participa- tion as are altruism and social identification associated with an increase in all types of political participation . Immigrants are also heavily involved in urban politics, often as opposed to formal and national politics, including partici- pation in local street life and engagement with the arts, often using media and communication technologies . Though Putnam blames declining social capital for lack of civic engagement by traditional means, he does find that citizens are increasingly likely to get involved in identity-based organizations, including non-profit advocacy groups. Individuals are also willing to get involved in political issues when they are likely to see a return on their investment of time and energy. This argument follows from the work of Olson , who found that where benefits to the individual are not so clear, people need selective incentives to participate in order to overcome the collective action problem. In some instances, however, though benefits may not be individualized, residents can see collective rewards. For example, in participatory budgeting processes in Brazil, Goldfrank found that people were more likely to continue to participate if they saw tangible results from their efforts, such as new sidewalks or streetlights in their neighborhoods. Further research identified similar effects in participatory budgeting processes in New York City . In a study of three Western European cities, Bologna, Stuttgart, and Zaragoza, Manes-Rossi . found that citizens were more likely to continue participating when they perceived their role as effec- tive in the co-production of outcomes. Still, other scholars have criticized the focus on community participation as the solution to urban conflicts and challenges of service delivery. David Harvey writes, One of the aspects of much contemporary debate about the urban which I find particularly striking is the tendency when faced with all sorts of difficulties again and again to reach into this bag called community , on the assumption that community is going to save us all. Community, endowed with salving powers, is perceived as capable of redeeming the mess which we are creating in our cities A lot of community construction projects are, in the end, a recipe for isolation. They isolate groups from the city as a whole. () Harvey recognizes that the community cannot be separated from the structure, logic, and institutions of the whole city, but when we rely on communities to solve local issues we often seem to forget this, leaving the individuals within a specific location responsible for the fate of their own development. Further, in global development literature, Cooke and Kothari make the case that participation represents a new tyranny in which the most powerful in communities often are the only voices to be heard, reinforcing existing dynamics. Additionally, they argue that local knowledge and decision-making processes are often overridden by outsiders who bring their own toolboxes to manage projects. In the U.S. context, marginalized groups also report what Sally Nuamah describes as a a type of mobilization fatigue that transpires when citizens engaged in policy processes are met with a lack of democratic transparency and responsive- Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. The role of participation in global world cities 291 ness despite high levels of repeated participation (2021: 1115). Assessing qualitative data from public meetings on school closures in low-income black neighborhoods, Nuamah finds negative perceptions of local government and collective participatory debt among partici- pants who feel they are used as tokens of representation rather than sincere partners. In sum, research has shown the basic difficulty of ensuring diverse and inclusive participa- tion of residents without over-burdening those who have the least time and resources to get involved, particularly when their involvement does not lead to meaningful input and progress in their communities and cities at large. Moving forward, there are plentiful opportunities for research on urban participation for the purpose of understanding why some residents do and do not participate over others and the resulting impact of these choices. We know that partici- pation matters for shaping the city when the loudest or wealthiest voices are heard. But when more diverse voices are involved in more genuine forms of policy making and deliberation with clear benefits for those who make the time and give their energy to engage, participation should be more likely to create the types of cities that meet the needs of broad populations. THE HOW OF PARTICIPATION Politics is defined by the contests between citizens for power. ipation, then, has primarily focused on how non-elites or those without formal powers of representation are able to gain influence for political and policy outcomes. But participation writ-large is often taken for granted as having an influence on outcomes without careful examination. As Burton states, for something that is held to be so important and to deliver a myriad of benefits, we know little of the extent to which the benefits of public participation are in fact delivered or of the balance of these benefits with any costs. In recent years, however, this has begun to change as urban scholars, in particular, seek to understand when and how individual and collective actions matter. Three areas in which scholarship has focused include, the role of coalitions, the rules of participatory processes, and the strategies of organizations to achieve their goals. COALITIONS Dahl argued that urban political power, as opposed to national, is fragmented and involves many different people from different backgrounds in decision making. But, even if this were true, scholars have been skeptical about the potential for citizen impact. Molotch (1976, 1979) and Logan and Molotch argued that growth machines, made up of pro-growth coalitions, make decisions about land use, public expenditures, and urban social life. These growth machines include public officials, the media, and union leadership to support projects of accumulation. The quest for profits and revenues limits the ability of outsiders, including non-elite citizens, to affect power over urban decision making. Clarence Stone reinforced the idea that citizens have limited impact through his study of Atlanta. He argued that coalitions develop between like-minded public, private, and civil society actors. Because the demands of low-income neighborhoods are not aligned with the quest for revenues and profits, citizen groups may make incremental progress but will never overcome the coalition of monied interests. In the era of globalization, Saskia Sassen added that Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. 2 Handbook of urban politics and policy global competition reinforces the power of elites to shape the policy agenda and outcomes. Castells also contended that the ability of transnational networks to wield power in cities desperate for revenues negates the needs and desires of urban residents. But in truth, evidence over the last two decades demonstrates that there is not uniformity across cities in how citizens impact politics and policies. We see many instances in which growth coalitions appear to win over neighborhood coalitions, but we also see instances in which groups of citizens can change the direction of policy outcomes. Jones-Correa and Wong describe the success of housing groups in NYC, labor rights in LA, and education groups in Detroit, and demonstrate the importance of resource mobilization and sustained agendas for long-term impact. Stone, himself, along with his co-authors also maintains that in the current era the lines between actors are no longer as rigid as when he wrote in the late 1980s. New framing of issues around equity and sustainability for long-term growth means greater recognition of the importance of investments in communities and neighborhoods and not just downtowns and corporations. In addition, Stone and Stoker relate the willing- ness of community organizations to bargain instead of protesting as a means to compromise and multi-faceted solutions. Moderation on all sides of urban development conflicts enables citizen groups to have greater impact on policies than in the past. In addition, scholars charge that coalitional theories of development do not necessarily apply when we look past the primary cities of the U.S. and Europe. Fox contends that both the growth machine approach and regime theory assume strong independence of municipal governments, which is not always the case, particularly in developing countries with a legacy of colonial governance . Further, writing about African cities, LeBas argues that although there is variation, party institutionalization does not exist in a similar way to the two-party system in the U.S. Without a strong party system, fractionalization may make coa- litions for development more precarious. Finally, the intentions of activists may vary across contexts. As Walton finds, some actors may display a preference for clientelism when benefits are worth loyalty, thereby prioritizing short-term gains over long-term advocacy objectives. This line of research reminds us that not all interests are as we presume, and local norms cannot be ignored in our analyses of outcomes. INSTITUTIONAL DESIGN IN PARTICIPATORY PROCESSES Participatory processes inherently take away power from public officials and entrenched interests, both of which may stymie the process by not really giving meaningful opportuni- ties for citizens to impact outcomes. Since the late 1960s questions regarding the design of participatory processes and later the institutional rules and norms of participatory institutions pose fruitful research avenues across political science, urban planning, public policy, public management, and development scholars. In 1969, Sherry Arnstein developed A Ladder of Citizen Participation, which sought to characterize different types of participatory processes and their resulting impact. At a time when municipal governance sits at the intersection of global and local, public and private actors, Arnstein provides a typology for how citizens are to participate to increase representa- tion of their interests and elicit accountability. At the bottom of the ladder, manipulation, a contrived or fake attempt to bring in citizens voices, and therapy, trying to change the people involved and not the program, are both akin to non-participation. These are the outcomes of Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. The role of participation in global world cities 293 public officials giving lip-service to the idea of participation without genuine interest or possi- bly even with mal-intent. In the middle, participatory processes that inform, consult, or placate offer a degree of tokenism, but without giving citizens significant decision-making power. At the top of the ladder, partnership, delegated power, and citizen control indicate the highest levels of community impact on planning and policy-making outcomes. While we see many examples of partnerships between public, private, and non-profit entities across cities, critics argue that citizen control can actually promote inefficient and balkanized public services with many questions arising over which groups deserve this power. In any case, the ladder was pivotal for demonstrating that not all participatory processes equally generate citizen impact and provided a typology for future researchers to label the types of processes they examined and measure the resulting impact. Comparative scholars have also conducted research on the implementation of participatory institutions, particularly for budgeting, in cities around the world. Meant to enable community members to directly decide spending priorities, participatory budgeting has traveled to over 1500 cities worldwide . The problem, according to Baiocchi and Ganuza is that the institutional innovations of the first instances of PB in Brazil have not translated to these processes around the world, often failing to ensure the participation of the most marginalized groups as originally intended. Wampler . argue that PB has been transferred to other contexts without the basic element of empowered decision making. Though Touchton and Wampler find that in Brazil PB is associated with improvements in the well-being of residents, these benefits are not guaranteed without dedicated public officials and rules that enable decision-making power of citizens. In fact, across cities in the U.S. and Europe, research finds the collaboration across stake- holders is key. Pateman finds in the U.S. context that though many individuals are hesitant to get involved in participatory budgeting, collaboration between public adminis- trators and citizens, which varies considerably across contexts, makes it easier for citizens to feel welcome and like their voices matter. In European cities, Manes-Rossi . find that the municipality must commit to genuine two-way dialogue for PB to generate co-production. Outside of PB, these same principles apply to other participatory governance institutions, including municipal councils for housing across Brazil . Archon Fung and Erik Olin Wright also find that participatory institutions need rough equality of power among participants from the beginning to be mechanisms of empowered participatory governance . Once again, influence is not guaranteed through the formalization of participation, but rather the state must agree to share power with citizens to affect impact. STRATEGIES Lastly, scholars have asked what types of engagement are most successful in achieving aims. The choices made by individuals and organizations of citizens in terms of goals, tactics, targets, and the means of influence should matter for their impact. Some findings for best practices are clear. For example, Liao and Ma argue that citizen participation needs to be carefully planned, coordinated, and supported by strategic partners. Ianniello . find that to be successful organizations need the support of officials and an organizational structure with clear community representation. Extended dialogue with public officials also Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. 2 Handbook of urban politics and policy enhances citizens knowledge of the issues and constraints around solutions faced by various stakeholders. In addition, the ideology of collective movements shapes the strategies they pursue and their ensuing impact. Most recently urban movements have sprung up all over the world under the banner of the right to the city. Like discussed above, Henri Lefebvre first argued in the 1960s that all residents should have the collective right to shape the many aspects of their city . As traced by Peter Marcuse , Lefebvre argued that the call for the right to the city could manifest in different ways, from demands for inclusion in the benefits of the city to outright transformation of policies and institutions to address the needs of the most mar- ginalized in society. How collective actors interpret the right to the city matters for the strate- gies they use and the goals they want to achieve. For example, a more conservative approach to the right to the city might include the demand for more subsidized housing to low-income residents while a more radical approach might call for a wholesale change in the provision of government housing in which low-income residents, themselves, define solutions and manage a collective process of decision making and program implementation. Harvey calls for the accumulation of dispossession in which low-income communities claim ownership over land they may have lived on for years, negating the power of elites to control capital in the city. Along these lines, Eleonora Pasotti studied 29 cases of anti-development protests in aspiring global cities, explaining how and why mobilization occurs and then why it is or is not successful. She finds that experiential tools significantly motivate mobilization, especially when combined with protest legacies and broad networks but these strategies have the most impact when protestors have strong allies in city government and there is not a right-wing alignment between the mayors and executives at the state and federal levels. The ideological motivation for strategies is key to success, but only when combined with allies in govern- ment. Donaghy finds similar outcomes in the study of community organizations for housing across cities in the U.S. and Brazil in which a radical ideology combined with aligned governments in power created the best opportunities for impact on generating and preserving affordable housing for low-income residents. In sum, research to date has found a number of variables that influence how participation impacts policy making, institutions, and public programs. The role of coalitions while not a given still may be critical in any context for understanding the barriers and opportunities for the ability of citizens to succeed in eliciting benefits and services or determining the path of urban development. In addition, we know that all forms of participatory processes are not the same in terms of the powers given to residents and the potential influence they may have. The more responsibilities given to citizens in participatory processes over a concerted time frame, the more bonds of trust are formed on all sides. And in the end, the strategies of collective actors in terms of the goals and tactics they pursue, are both determined by their ideology and relationship with the state and determine the strength of their impact on urban development outcomes. CURRENT STATE AND FUTURE OF THE FIELD It may seem hyperbolic, but to understand the role of participation in shaping cities that meet the needs of citizens may very well mean the difference between life and death. Whether citizens have access to clean water, dignified housing, and quality education and are able to Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. The role of participation in global world cities 295 mitigate and adapt to the impacts of climate change requires individuals and groups to demand more of cities, states, and national governments currently unable or unwilling to provide solu- tions. Because of these very real, imminent challenges facing cities the focus on local politics has only increased in recent decades. Trounstine argued that local politics has been marginalized as a subfield in previous decades. But apart from the ethical reasons for studying participation in cities, she cites three methodological factors that motivate increasing attention to the local and urban levels of study. First, there are a great number of political events happening at the local level from which to draw our cases. Second, cities offer methodological advantages in terms of diversity, variety, and number of cases, and third, cities generate very different questions than looking at the national level. In essence, we need to understand how and why cities are unique entities and the number of diverse cases provides a seemingly never-ending supply of events, institutions, policies, and populations from which to choose. Given the wealth of available data and the number of cities within and across national borders, the study of participation in urban politics is not constrained to one methodology. While many studies rely on survey and electoral data for quantitative analysis related to who participates in politics, scholars are also applying these methods to the study of protests, com- munity mobilization, and individual contact with representatives, among many other types of participation, assisted by data available online through social media, non-profit advocacy groups, and government agencies. Still, in-depth case studies of participation are often a pre- ferred method of scholars wishing to make causal inferences between activities and outcomes, and as noted above, more recently scholars have begun to look across cities that vary by soci- oeconomic characteristics for broader generalizations or idiosyncrasies in urban politics (for example, Pasotti 2020 and Donaghy 2018). This emphasis on understanding causal relationships between participation and outcomes fits within the approach of critical urban theory, which as Neil Brenner states, emphasizes the politically or ideologically mediated, socially contested and therefore malleable character of urban space that is, its continual construction as a site, medium, and outcome of his- torically specific relations of power . Critical urban theory takes on a somewhat normative position as to the goals of democracy and equality as it insists that another, more democratic, socially just, and sustainable form of urbanization is possible, even if such possi- bilities are currently being suppressed through dominant institutional arrangements, practices, and ideologies (Brenner 2012: 11). Brenner, Marcuse, and Mayer argue that the economic crisis of 2008 2009 intensi- fied unrest against capitalism across the globe and our goal now should be to understand how each city fits within the system of capital accumulation. Cities are the sites where conflicts over accumulation take place and research is needed to understand both the site-specific processes of urban restructuring as well as comparisons of how conflicts emerge and possibly resolve across cities. Along these lines, Brenner, Marcuse, and Mayer suggest research pro- jects to uncover: systematic injustices, exclusions, and marginalizations; the balance of social forces; and the lines of conflict. Recently, scholars have also argued for the application of American political development to urban politics, i.e. urban political development . Building on the approach of studying ideas, interests, and institutions over time, Weaver defines UPD as driven by intercurrent operation of multiple urban political orders, which are comprised of political coalitions, driven by overarching purposes (or ideas) that capture control of governing institu- Handbook of Urban Politics and Policy, edited by Ronald K. Vogel, Edward Elgar Publishing Limited, 2024. ProQuest Ebook Central, Created from torontomu on 2025-01-15 . Copyright 2024. Edward Elgar Publishing Limited. All rights reserved. 2 Handbook of urban politics and policy tions. To understand the direction of change within cities, then, we need to study the ideology or ideas that guide current actors and not just the economics of growth. Urban scholars may use the UPD framework to guide their narratives of how and when cities change over time given the ideas, interests, and institutions in place to either confine or motivate activities and impact. As urban research proceeds in the coming years, both critical urban theory and urban political development, among other approaches utilizing quantitative and qualitative methods will continue to intermingle and contribute to our understanding of the role of participation in cities. We can expect that comparative urbanism will examine how different cities are positioned within global economic and political dynamics, how various forms of governance interact to create openings for citizen influence, and how the formal and informal norms of cities create power and voice. Moving forward scholars need to recognize and study the diverse ways that urban residents participate in politics and why some means of participation appeal more to some groups than others. Post Covid-19, the study of the use of technology for interactive participation between citizens and government will likely also grow as will the question of how crises affect participation. Crises including displacement of people across borders, climate change, housing affordability, and disease epidemics threaten the very operation of urban governments and necessitate comprehensive solutions from citizens of all classes, ethnicities, races, ages, and genders. CONCLUSIONS How we define participation, who participates, and how citizen engagement is carried out all influence when citizen participation matters for shaping cities to meet the needs of residents now and into the future. Scholars have defined participation in numerous ways, and as we look beyond the cities of the U.S. and Western Europe, we continue to reevaluate from whose perspective we are labeling political participation and what those labels mean for how we consider who participates and the impact of their activities. In terms of who participates as we generally define the term, we know that those who are wealthier, white, and male tend to be more engaged in formal governmental processes, though as issues of gentrification, school violence, housing, and other local challenges impact marginalized communities and individuals, we see increasing activism among marginalized populations though scholars still need to work on understanding how participatory processes can work to engage diverse residents. Neither coalitions, nor participatory institutions, nor the strategies of urban-based organizations alone are the key to actions that make a difference to the direction of cities, but sharing power and cooperating across government and community actors in multiple ways can lead to change. At the end of the day, sitting through long meetings, standing in line to vote, marching in the hot sun or cold rain, have to matter or citizens will become less rather than more engaged. And in cities, whose voices are heard in the halls of power as well as how communities create change within their own spaces will shape the course of urban development. Scholars will need to work diligently to keep up with the changing relationships among actors to provide vital answers to when citizen participation matters. Please do not redistribute CHAPTER Future Prospects for Municipal Governments T he future for local governments in Canada will be more activist. Frances Frisken once noted that Canadian municipalities often used the lack of jurisdictional authority as an excuse for inaction. 1 The past decade has suggested a significant attitudinal change in such local governmental thinking. Examples, large and small, abound-from the commitment to \"fair trade\" in Wolfville, Nova Scotia, to the establishment of North America's first official supervised injection site in Vancouver, British Columbia. Leading up to the 2015 federal election, Canada's municipalities stepped forward offering to take and help settle Syrian (and related) refugees- though municipal fiscal reality meant that many local governments acknowledged that their offers were partly contingent on federal immi- grant settlement aid. On environmental matters, many municipalities have signed on to Kyoto targets on emissions, not waiting for senior governments to lead with their own action. Municipalities, such as Burnaby, BC, have indicated they will take whatever local action they can-including civil disobedience-to stop the development of Kinder Morgan's Trans Mountain Pipeline and oil sands bitumen shipping in their communities. Apart from the designation as Canada's \"best run municipality,\" Burnaby Mayor Derek Corrigan and his fellow Burnaby Citizens' Association councillors stand out as dedicated, committed, local representatives and as electoral examples with a history of democratic success through this local engagement. 1 Frances Frisken, ed., The Changing Canadian Metropolis: A Public Policy Perspective, Introduction, Berkeley, CA: Institute of Governmental Studies Press, University of California, 1994. 3 In 2014-2018, British Columbia became the last Canadian province to institute four-year terms of office; given the senior trend of fixed- usually four-year-terms provincially and federally, this longer local governmental planning horizon may push the political professionalism of councillors and councils from a more part-time orientation. While sometimes adding recruitment challenges for smaller municipalities, the shift to longer council terms also raises questions about ensuring accountability, a feature largely answered with the development of local political parties. While for most Canadian local governments, this latter development remains uncertain, there is no doubt that local governments in Canada will be more engaged. How well municipalities fare in the 21st century will depend, as always, on how successfully they maintain a balance between their two primary roles: representative political and administrative service delivery (see Box 12.1). The tension between these two roles is reflected in two contrasting images of municipal government-as a democratic institution that responds to community concerns, and as but one of many players in a competitive marketplace in which individual interests prevail. This book began with fanciful suggestions about the 21st century belonging to Canada's municipalities. What have we learned in the more than 400 intervening pages that support or detract from this optimistic outlook? NEl Chapter 1 Future Prospects for Municipal Governments 3 What needs to happen if this new century is to bring greater municipal significance? Taking a New Look The best way to start, especially for those in municipal government, is to turn upside down the usual way of looking at municipalities, a perspec- tive that has them on the bottom of several levels of higher jurisdiction, as in Figure 12.1. According to this traditional view, municipalities are subordinate and subservient creatures of the provinces to which they owe their existence. They are also dependent on the federal (and provincial) levels for financial assistance that-in practice-is limited and elusive, and they are significantly affected by senior federal government actions (and inaction). They are also increasingly constrained by international agreements and organizations and by global competitive pressures and a prevailing neoliberal ideological paradigm. There is, however, an alternative way of looking at municipalities and their place in the world, unaccustomed as we are to considering it. What if we invert the traditional hierarchical view, as in Figure 12.2? What if we see local governments as the first order of government? Let us con- sider the significance of such a reversal. Globalization and the accompanying free trade regime are certainly forces to be reckoned with, but we shouldn't overstate their impor- tance for day-to-day municipal operations. Not all firms are completely mobile, and most would think long and hard before pulling up stakes and setting up shop in another country where culture, language, and other barriers may present quite a challenge. Since the terrorist attacks Figure 12.1 The Traditional Hierarchy International Agreements and Organizations I I I Federal Provincial Government Goverment I Municipal Government Figure 12.2 The Inverted Hierarchy Local Citizens I I Provincial Government I Federal Government I International Agreements and Organizations of September 11, 2001, many actions have been rationalized or justified \"because of 9 11\" or \"ISIS\" and its equivalents-no matter how tenuous the link. The attacks have become an all too convenient cover or excuse for actions (especially adversely affecting individual rights and free- doms-as in Canada's C-5 Anti-Terrorism Act of 2015) that would not otherwise be tolerated. In much the same way, globalization is constantly invoked as the reason why something must be done or cannot be done. Without in any way denying its importance, we should not allow it to become an excuse for municipal actions or inaction. There is still consid- erable scope for municipal initiative within a global world. It is instructive to examine Martin Horak's analysis of the background to Toronto's New Deal campaign that ultimately led to the new City of Toronto Act of June 2006. 2 At first blush, one might interpret this devel- opment as the inevitable response of a global city seeking out additional powers to strengthen its competitive position. But Horak found that the forces of global economic competition were not the key factors driving 2 Martin Horak, Governance Reform from Below: Multilevel Politics and the \"New Deal\" Campaign in Toronto, Global Dialogue Series, , Nairobi, ON-Habitat, 2008, accessed October 30, 2011 and July 3, 2015, at aspx?publicationiD 2494. A similar dynamic might occur among Prime Minister Justin Trudeau, Premier Kathleen Wynne, and Mayor John Tory for Toronto. Future Prospects for Municipal Governments 3 Toronto's New Deal campaign. Instead, he identified a number of devel- opments that came together to create the impetus and opportunity for change: The provincially imposed amalgamation that created the City ofT oronto; Provincial downloading of social programs that burdened the new municipality with substantial costs; and Elections at all three levels of government that resulted in leaders (Prime Minister Martin, Premier McGuinty, and Mayor Miller) predisposed to working together. The size of the amalgamated municipality gave it increased clout, and the disparities in downloaded cost burdens between the new City of Toronto and the suburban municipalities in the surrounding Greater Toronto Area provided further stimulus for those seeking greater powers for Toronto. The arrival of the \"three Ms\" as heads of the three levels of government provided the other major factor favourable to a New Deal for Toronto. Horak's research into the Toronto case demonstrates that even in cities that are highly integrated into the global economy, the exigencies of global competition are not the only or necessarily even the primary influence. He concludes, \"both the size of the new City and the sense of fiscal crisis that accompanied its birth were critical to the emergence of the New Deal campaign, in that they enabled and encour- aged the new City to move beyond the passive intergovernmental stance that Canadian municipalities have typically assumed given their weak standing in the constitutional order.\" 3 Once again, we have a welcome example of what \"eager beavers\" can accomplish. Though Meaghan Joy and Ronald Vogel offer caution in overinterpretation of the Toronto advantage. 4 Because the constraints imposed by globalization apply more to federal and provincial governments, it can even be argued that the net result is to liberate rather than to restrict municipalities. National borders are more porous and the nation state is challenged by international trade rules and agencies and scaled back by neoliberal preachings about the inherent superiority of private-sector operations. These global and ideological pressures affect the local level as well, but municipalities also have an opportunity to move into the vacuum created at the centre by the reduced role of the federal and provincial levels. In fact, the municipal capacity to capitalize on this opportunity has been strengthened, unwittingly, by the actions taken by the federal and provincial levels in response to global- ization and neoliberalism. This issue is rapidly evolving. In addition to our own monitoring and analysis, Alberta Municipalities is engaged with the Government of Alberta and the Federation of Canadian Municipalities to align and amplify messaging where possible. We will explore the ways in which the new U.S. administration's actions will affect municipalities in the weeks and months ahead and at our Spring Municipal Leaders Caucus on March 6 in Edmonton. As your municipality is considering the impacts of tariffs on your community, some of the potential impacts could include: Reduced funding from the Government of Alberta Particularly because less taxable revenue will be generated from natural resource exports, which is what drives the provincial government s surplus or deficit budget position. Funding provided to municipalities via the Local Government Fiscal Framework is directly tied to the provincial government s revenues. Therefore, reductions in provincial revenue will result in less funding to municipalities. Reduced municipal revenues For example, higher unemployment will affect residents ability to access pay-for- use municipal services (e.g., transportation, recreation facilities). Reduced Investment Tariffs may cause businesses to slow or shutdown their operations (e.g., farming, agriculture, energy), along with limiting new economic development in municipalities. Stunted property tax growth Higher unemployment rates and increasing costs of living may increase pressure from residents to maintain lower property tax rates. Higher costs on goods and services In addition to tariffs goods, alternatives (including those produced in Canada) may be more expensive, resulting in municipalities having higher-than-planned costs for goods and services. There will also be instances where Canadian-made products become cheaper due to reduced demand (i.e., countervailing forces). Supply chain disruptions and delays Demand is likely to be affected for both international and domestic producers, Feb 19 2 0 2 5 Fair Funding for Strong Communities LEARN MORE. causing issues for the availability and accessibility of goods and services. Increased demand for social supports Recessionary economic factors may create (and exacerbate) issues pertaining to homeless, in-need , and new temporary Canadian populations (e.g., access to medical services, policing services). Cookie settings HIDE","preparedAudioPaths":"[\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-0-1775640057480.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-1-1775640070371.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-2-1775640083235.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-3-1775640084982.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-4-1775640086519.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-5-1775640088070.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-6-1775640089767.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-7-1775640091416.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-223-chunk-8-1775640092706.mp3\"]","preparedAt":"2026-04-08T09:21:33.506Z","createdAt":"2026-04-06T08:43:35.115Z"},{"id":224,"originalName":"Let’s empower municipalities, too often the little siblings of federalism.pdf","displayName":"Let’s empower municipalities, too often the little siblings of federalism.pdf","objectPath":"/local/uploads/1775479415356-Let_s_empower_municipalities__too_often_the_little_siblings_of_federalism.pdf","contentType":"application/pdf","size":1183647,"folder":"week-11-cppa122-reading","listened":false,"lastChunkIndex":0,"totalChunks":7,"checkedChunks":null,"ttsAudioUrl":null,"ttsGeneratedAt":null,"extractedText":"Let s empower municipali- ties, too often the little sib- lings of federalism Municipalities deserve to be put on firmer footing with more control over revenue sources and more fiscal flexi- bility, treated as full partners. JUNE 9, 2 BY ENID SLACK TOMAS HACHARD Saskatoon, Sask. Shutterstock.com Municipalities have played a central role in Canada s response to the COVID-19 pandemic. They have provided economic relief to residents and businesses, instituted public health measures, delivered vaccines and begun planning for a post-pandemic future. The increased cost of this work, combined with decreased revenues particularly from user fees such as transit fares have resulted in significant, unanticipated operating deficits. Large cities with transit systems have been especially hard hit. Because municipalities are required by law to pass balanced operating budgets every year, they have not been able to resort to more borrowing. Instead, federal and provincial governments have stepped in to help them cover the shortfalls. Alongside fiscal deficits, municipalities have also faced a governance deficit. While they have been on the front lines responding to the pandemic, they nevertheless have been excluded in many cases from key decisions made at the federal and provincial level. Instead, municipalities have relied on advocacy to get heard, although public health units have in some cases made use of their authority to enact certain restrictions. All of this points to an underlying reality about Canadian federalism. Despite the important role that cities play in the federation, local governments are equipped with limited sources of revenue largely property taxes, user fees, and transfers from the federal and provincial governments. They also do not have any real role or voice in Canada s federal structure and are generally not included in federal- provincial discussions or negotiations. Both of these problems need to be addressed. Municipalities should have access to the revenue necessary to fund the services they must provide. There should also be regular trilateral meetings among Ottawa, the provinces and senior municipal leaders to address issues of common concern, while recognizing the different needs of large and small municipalities. Municipalities have too often been the forgotten partners or little siblings of Canadian federalism. Recent research has highlighted the challenges Canadian municipalities face in this respect. Municipalities have a semblance of authority in several policy areas, but often little actual power to make changes unilaterally, partly because provinces have shown little interest in ceding control. They have inadequate revenue sources and insufficient fiscal flexibility to meet their responsibilities. There is often unclear and overlapping jurisdiction between municipalities, provinces and the federal government. Finally, much of Canada lacks appropriate regional governance structures to encourage inter-municipal co-operation. Coming out of the pandemic, there is an opportunity to put municipalities and Canada on firmer footing by addressing fiscal capacity and flexibility, embracing municipalities as partners and taking municipal differences into consideration. Address fiscal capacity and flexibility Provincial governments determine the revenue sources available to municipalities. They also hold municipalities to balanced budgets for operating purposes, and restrict how much they can borrow for capital expenditures. This high degree of provincial control ensures that municipalities maintain good fiscal health, but it can obfuscate real fiscal problems. Balanced budgets, for instance, can hide the fact that a municipality is unable to pay the cost of maintaining and building new infrastructure, including public transit and affordable housing, using revenue sources at their disposal. The limited number of revenue sources available to municipalities also means they lack fiscal flexibility. If transfers from government are adjusted downward, or property tax revenues decrease, they have few other places to turn for revenue. Without a mix of taxes, they are less able to respond to shifts in the economy, evolving demographics and changing expenditure needs. In this respect, big cities in Canada compare unfavourably to the rest of the world. In a study of eight international cities, only London was found to have access to fewer taxation options than Toronto. In response, a reconsideration of who does what in Canada particularly with respect to provincial-municipal relations is needed. Such a process should ensure two things. Saint John, N.B. Shutterstock.com First, municipalities should have revenues that allow them to deliver effectively This may entail giving municipalities access to new revenue sources, or uploading responsibilities to the provincial level. Second, municipalities should have appropriate revenue sources for the services they deliver. For instance, both in response to provincial downloading of responsibilities and to resident demands for action on issues like the opioid crisis and mental health, municipalities are increasingly being tasked with redistributive services such as social housing, public health and social assistance. These are best funded by progressive taxes, such as an income tax or a sales tax with mitigation for low-income individuals. Embrace municipalities as partners A new vision for municipalities in Canada should also address intergovernmental relations. In Canada, there is no formal mechanism for ongoing, formal federal-provincial- This is despite the fact that decisions taken by provincial and federal governments on immigration, infrastructure and public health, to name a few examples, have significant effects on municipalities. In cases where there is insufficient funding for programs, municipalities often bear the cost of filling gaps in services. Even in cases where municipalities have a semblance of authority for instance, land-use planning and elections provinces can often second-guess and or overrule their decisions. Many of Canada s policy challenges require increased co-ordination and co- operation among all three orders of government. As Gabriel Eidelman has noted, Urban governance is not simply municipal governance. Urban policy-making necessarily involves all levels of government. Municipalities should not need to wait to see what arrangements are made by the provinces and Ottawa in areas with municipal impact. Just as the provinces and the federal government meet regularly, without regard to whether there are particular issues that need to be discussed, so should cities, provinces and the federal government have formal avenues for regular meetings and collaborative governance. Instituting more formal trilateral relations, however, runs up against the issue of fragmentation. Canada has approximately 3,750 local governments. It is unfeasible to involve them all in intergovernmental discussions. Ensuring that Canada s major economic regions have robust metropolitan governance structures through which municipalities can co-ordinate on shared policy challenges and, when possible, speak as one voice, can help address this issue. Take municipal differences into consideration Canada is a largely urban and suburban country. Nevertheless, around a quarter of Canadians live in a municipality with a population of less than 20,000. Small and large municipalities are distinct in many ways. Larger municipalities generally can draw more revenue from their tax base and also have a larger and more sophisticated public service. As a result, policies and decisions tailored to large cities may not work for smaller municipalities. Instead, asymmetrical arrangements may be preferable. This might mean large cities and metropolitan regions take on more responsibility and gain access to new revenue sources, while smaller municipalities stay as they are and rely more on transfers. In such a scenario, greater disparities may emerge among municipalities, so new or enhanced provincial-municipal equalization transfers may also be required to ensure that all municipalities can provide at least an average level of local public services by levying an average standard tax rate. COVID-19 has shown us once more that municipalities play a fundamental role in the country s policy-making. It is equally apparent that municipalities will be critical to address climate change and tackle the affordable housing crisis, among other challenges. Reforming our fiscal and governance structures would be the next step in reflecting the key role of municipalities in the federation. This article is part of the Reshaping Canada s Cities After the Pandemic Shockwave special feature. Enid Slack Enid Slack is the director of the Institute on Municipal Finance and Governance at the Munk School of Global Affairs and Public Policy at the University of Toronto. MORE FROM THIS AUTHOR Tomas Hachard Tomas Hachard is manager of programs and research at the Institute on Municipal Finance and Governance. How are Canadian municipal leaders expected to lead when the future is so unclear? Perhaps it is best to look at what we do know and extrapolate from there. Keynesian economics tells us that the best way to restart and sustain a battered economy has been public spending, bolstered by public borrowing. In response to the COVID-19 shutdown of the economy, we have seen federal debt balloon to the highest it has been in decades. That effort, complemented by provincial and municipal fiscal initiatives, and unprecedented infusions of credit from the Bank of Canada, headed-off an economic disaster for millions of Canadians. But it will leave a legacy. In the short term, government support is positioned to cushion municipal operating deficits caused by extraordinary expenditures and revenue losses in 2020. This support includes the recent 19 billion Safe Restart Agreement announcement, which in the case of Ontario s 7 billion share will include 2 billion for Ontario municipalities and 2 billion for public transit authorities. Many local leaders wonder what levels of support will be like for 2021 as they begin their budget planning process, with significant question marks about both the revenue and cost side for next year. Longer-term, it may result in renewed efforts to boost infrastructure spending, if capital budgets at all three levels of government can be preserved. Before the end of this municipal council term in Ontario and elsewhere in Canada, the fiscal hangover will lead to belt-tightening, coupled with efforts to ensure that the economy grows sustainably and that further public spending is constrained. As we know from history, when constraint is adopted by other orders of government, the prospects for significant financial help for municipal governments from traditional sources will dim. Municipalities will all be urged, in UK journalist Ann Leslie s colourful phrase, to kill your own snakes . If there was ever any doubt before the pandemic, we are now firmly facing a structural deficit, particularly in local government finance. Much is anticipated for the new normal . We await a COVID-19 vaccine and the reinventing of the complex global supply chain for the goods and services that we use (and produce) every day. Some developments are with us to stay, such as remote working and telecommuting, more online retail and on-street product fulfillment, social distancing, and the increasing vagaries of a global marketplace. Many of these developments had been predicted but were expected to happen over the course of a few decades, not months. It now appears that we may face a municipal version of Moore s Law (which in the Information Technology field has accurately predicted a disruptive doubling of technological capacity every 18 months). In parallel with the pandemic, we have also seen a global response to the death of George Floyd. Public demonstrations have led to demands for fundamental reform of policing. The Black Lives Matter movement has spurred re-thinking societal issues such as racial discrimination and response to calls for emergency assistance for incidents related to mental illness, addictions, and domestic violence. As these economic, fiscal, and social developments continue, both from COVID- 19 and other factors, some things are certain like the continuing financial challenges facing municipalities while we may also see pressure: To increase productivity, to be more tax-competitive and to reduce public sector costs, which means reduced labour costs To accommodate autonomous vehicles, ride sharing, smart transportation, and more so-called active transportation To absorb the land-use and tax impacts of fundamental changes to retail, office, parking requirements, and new models of housing for the elderly To address environmental sustainability, housing costs and social inequality in our communities and in our workplaces on an accelerated timetable To reform policing and society s response to emergency calls-for-service for incidents related to mental illness, addictions, and families in crisis; and, Making the leap to 5G mobile digital service delivery. All of this, while simultaneously lifting the burdens of cost and uncertainty caused by traditional regulation and approvals and borne by those to whom we are looking to create and sustain employment and to revitalize economic activity. So where does all this leave municipal governments? What does the future hold? What items need to be on the agenda of municipal governments? There are at least eight likely outcomes. Two are outlined below and another six will be addressed in a subsequent article: The municipal workforce will transform, as artificial-intelligence and machine- learning applications and monitoring technologies are used in all manner of repetitive or inspection-related processes, especially those guided by rules, established policies, standard conditions, and precedents. Just as asphalt-laying crews and meter-readers were displaced by paving machines and automated meter reading in the 20 century, technology will improve productivity in labour-intensive functions. Whether it is incremental change, like replacing site visits with digital video inspections, or more structural change, driven by AI and remote-monitoring technologies. Process reforms will produce quicker, more consistent, and less idiosyncratic decisions on everything from business licensing and re-zonings to asset management and inspections. Mirroring the banking and travel industries, which have displaced much of their routine in-person interaction with clients, municipal workforces must reskill. Fewer but more productive municipal employees will serve an increasingly larger and more sophisticated online clientele. The workforce that remains should be more diverse, in all aspects of that term. Fortunately, the 21 century needs of municipalities will make diversity objectives much more achievable than they were with a 20 century workforce built around seniority, fixed employee benefits, employment guarantees, and traditional skills. Finally, municipal governments should adopt the so-called commissioning model in the UK and EU, a model described in an excellent 2017 article by Taylor and Migone, then at Institute of Public Administration of Canada. With commissioning , municipal governments play their role by ensuring that services and infrastructure are available on reasonable terms to their communities, their residents and their businesses, rather than assuming they must be provided by direct, monopoly delivery employing municipal facilities and municipal workers, financed by taxpayers. New models of policing and public safety Nineteenth century models of policing and fire suppression will need to evolve delivery models that reflect today s public safety priorities. For example, responding to preponderant incidence of health-related emergencies, especially as the Baby Boomer cohort ages. These new delivery models will also be needed to address social, mental illness, substance abuse, and family crisis situations with responses more suitable for those calls for emergency assistance. Rather than defunding police, we can expect to see measures to produce a new workplace culture in policing. This new culture should begin with embedding within the police workforce staff who have other professional perspectives on dealing with public safety and calls for service. Modernizing policing will need to reflect four pillars of reform: Becoming a learning organization that aligns its values and leadership priorities throughout the staff complement, and dissipates the informal education of front-line constables Re-engineering the hierarchical structure of police organizations to reflect contemporary priorities (mental health issues, technological advances, etc) A risk-based approach to planning and staff deployment, with a focus on community collaboration, public health and social services (e.g., the comprehensive approach successfully employed by Karyn McCluskey with violent crime and gangs in Scotland) ; and Despite reallocation of some police resources and the introduction of new contemporary priorities, preserving within policing a robust capacity for th st th effective law enforcement and criminal investigations, with a focus on value- for-investment, outputs and outcomes. We could also see an overdue and successful societal effort, led by municipal councils with recently announced federal support, to reduce the level of urban handgun possession. Success in treating the scourge of handgun deaths and injuries, as more of a public health issue than a criminal matter, would certainly change the character and reduce the risk-profile of many urgent responses by both police and other agencies. In our next article, we will look at another six developments on the post-2020 action agenda for municipal governments. Dr. Robert P.
