Insights Phone 1-866-231-6 Careers Privacy Policy Accessibility Contact 24 7 Crisis response 2 StrategyCorp A Canadian Company","preparedAudioPaths":"[\"/tts-audio/prep-225-chunk-0-1775640130200.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-225-chunk-1-1775640132022.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-225-chunk-2-1775640133575.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-225-chunk-3-1775640135177.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-225-chunk-4-1775640136868.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-225-chunk-5-1775640138610.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-225-chunk-6-1775640140291.mp3\",\"/tts-audio/prep-225-chunk-7-1775640142295.mp3\"]","preparedAt":"2026-04-08T09:22:23.096Z","createdAt":"2026-04-06T08:43:35.539Z"},{"id":226,"originalName":"Municipal Priorities for 2020 and Beyond_ Part Two - StrategyCorp.pdf","displayName":"Municipal Priorities for 2020 and Beyond_ Part Two - StrategyCorp.pdf","objectPath":"/local/uploads/1775479415693-Municipal_Priorities_for_2020_and_Beyond__Part_Two_-_StrategyCorp.pdf","contentType":"application/pdf","size":140661,"folder":"week-11-cppa122-reading","listened":false,"lastChunkIndex":0,"totalChunks":8,"checkedChunks":null,"ttsAudioUrl":null,"ttsGeneratedAt":null,"extractedText":"Municipal Priorities for 2020 and Beyond: Part Two August 17, 2 Economy and Jobs, Institute By Michael Fenn and John Matheson, with contributions from members of In our recent article, our review of the impact of the events of 2020 ended with the questions: Where does this leave municipal governments? What does the future hold? What items need to be on the action agenda of municipal governments? We said that there were at least eight developments on the near- term horizon, and we described two in Part One (changes to the municipal workforce and new models of policing and public safety). There are at least six other sets of priorities for you to consider. New directions in community planning and housing The nature of land use planning in Canada will need to meet new imperatives. Working remotely seems positioned to be the new normal for many employers. If employees have the freedom to work from wherever they choose, where will they choose to live, if jobs are no longer the reason people cluster in large urban centres. Policy alone will not constrain people using more employment options and improved inter urban transit to seek better housing value and prices outside major urban centres. If remote work is here to say, will municipalities try to attract a new generation to move away from cities? What inducements and amenities will they offer businesses and millennial families? How should major urban centres respond? Municipalities and provincial authorities will need to accept that facilitating de concentration is a higher priority than trying to contain sprawl . But how do they do it by revitalizing slow growth communities and while preserving our agriculture, natural heritage, and urban core vitality? A new look for transportation and mobility With the post COVID impact of remote work and de concentration, we may see highways and city streets with less gridlock. Roads may still be busy but may be below design capacity. However, single passenger automobiles may increasingly be displaced by a wave of new types of transportation, especially if a vaccine or mitigation measures make it safer to share vehicles. As automated vehicles, ride sharing, ride hailing (e.g. Uber) and vehicle sharing make up a greater share of commuting and travel, we will need less space allocated to parking individual motor vehicles, both at home and at community commercial centres. Automobile and pick up truck ownership may decline. Gas station sites and car dealerships and plazas with acres of roadside parking will be progressively displaced by innocuous rapid charging stations, internet-based vehicle acquisition, and retail formats not focused on shoppers arriving in their own cars. Anticipating this liberation of stale land uses, municipalities should plan to free up prime urban real estate locations for creative place making. Rapid transit will be increasingly automated and integrated, along with its fare paying regimes and fare media . In public transit, many under patronized suburban bus routes and rapid transit feeder bus systems should be supplanted by a range of jitneys and ride hailing services, overcoming a barrier to increased transit use by reducing the elapsed time to the train station or other major destinations. If municipal governments manage the transformation creatively, it will expand mobility options for workers, adolescents, seniors, the economically disadvantaged, the disabled and remote residents. Existing zoning by laws and density targets should be re evaluated to meet the market driven housing demands of Baby Boomers and Gen X families, neither of whom seem to favour dense, costly, urban core houses and condominiums. In the case of aging seniors and those with chronic disease, provincial and municipal land use planning, housing, and healthcare strategies should aim to avoid both hospitalization and traditional models of long term care, while new models of seniors housing and nursing care evolve. Renewing local democracy and public engagement Digitally enabled public engagement has been a novel feature of the lockdown for municipal councils and for their efforts to reach their constituents. In fact, digitization has had a salutary effect in many instances, enhancing the quality of local democracy. Online engagement with the public allows a broader consultation in real time, discouraging the occasional theatrics of traditional public meetings, while ensuring that a broader range of voices can be heard. Online meetings dilute the loudest voice effect at the public forum. They also appear to favour more digitally assisted advance preparation by decision makers. Digital engagement overcomes one of the fundamental shortcomings in contemporary public consultation, since citizens who are disenfranchised, commuters, busy parents, or the peripherally involved, can participate equally and equitably. In other words, this digitally enabled wider consultation helps to balance the vested interest (like NIMBY) against the broader public interest, highlighting more diffuse benefits that might accrue to the community. All of this makes it quicker and easier to organize and hold public meetings, with a corresponding improvement in the timeliness (and often the quality) of decisions. As we have seen with the Black Lives Matter movement, it also facilitates social media driven marshalling of like minded people to demonstrate in response to events and to advocate for reform, often without requiring formal organizational structure or traditional issue leadership. Rather than rewarding delay and elaborate process, the new public engagement and digital decision making puts a premium on informed, evidence supported action. It can deliver refreshingly decisive, timely decision making, although it does risk the reduced accountability and due diligence born of haste. That is not to say it is not without its challenges. Many lament the loss of human contact, the ability to read the room, and to facilitate corridor discussions to bridge between the differences of parties. Most agree, however, that we have come a long way in just a few months. As familiarity with the use of digital meeting software grows through the population, the potential for their use in municipal contexts continues to grow. Rather than returning to past practices after the 2020 crises ebb, municipalities should continue to embrace and improve the use of the tools they have pioneered. Local democracy will be better for it. New fiscal realities Conventional on street or in mall retail will be in steep decline, and many industrial, hospitality, entertainment and office commercial enterprises will shrink in numbers, size, and significance. The real estate foundation of the municipal tax base must evolve. If not, municipalities face the vice grip of a declining non- residential tax base and an inability to tax growing segments of their local regional economy, notably global digital enterprises (which have small property assessment footprints). The municipal world needs to advocate for a better fiscal framework, based on the new economy. Development levies will inevitably play less of a role in rebuilding existing infrastructure to higher, more resilient standards, and in building new types of digital and transportation infrastructure to serve the whole community. Municipal fiscal plans and municipal accounting will need to consider more consistently the funding and financing infrastructure over its useful life, based on life cycle costing and beneficiary contributions, as well as employing well designed public private partnerships (P3s) as options. A new style of municipal service delivery Municipal service delivery patterns for both hard services and soft services will increasingly reflect their regional nature and the regional footprint of the everyday lives of their citizens and clients, as will efforts to attract employment creating investment to the economic region. From stormwater to emergency response to social inequality, a regional catchment area and economies of scale will be needed to address increasingly severe incidents like storm events and health emergencies, as well as the corrosive effects of economic disparity, unsupportable housing costs and a discouraging business environment. These events clearly ignore local municipal boundaries and they can only be addressed with an integrated regional approach. Responding to these new service delivery imperatives calls for new delivery and governance models. To reduce the inherent limitations of any monopoly, the UK and EU have pioneered the practice of commissioning public services, whether delivered by public bodies or contracted entities. A commissioning model allows a public authority to hold responsible those who deliver services, ensuring that they meet performance objectives, failing which they can mandate service improvements or change delivery agents. Municipalities should adopt this approach, as a key set of tools to achieve their other post 2020 priorities. A new, fit to purpose structure of local government An increasingly unsustainable municipal tax base may also drive municipal and provincial governments into a more fundamental and overdue re evaluation of the roles played by local government. If so, the discussion should not begin with simply a who does what reassignment of functional and fiscal responsibilities, as in the past. It should begin with a determination of which responsibilities of government are best planned, managed and delivered by local governments, and then funding them appropriately and sustainably in the new economy. That re examination should extend to consideration of the current and traditional range of local government functions and services, to ensure that obsolescent roles are phased out, current roles redefined, and emerging roles are anticipated and embraced proactively. After 2020, a prominent consideration in any re evaluation of responsibilities will be distinguishing and coordinating the roles of municipal government and the public health care system. This will include a realistic assessment of their respective abilities to fund their responsibilities in fields like long term care, hospital capital funding, public health, physician recruitment in under served areas, land ambulance, and social and children s services. Only after these determinations are made, there should be an evaluation of the ability of existing local government structures to do the jobs they are asked to do: form (and funding) should follow function. Conclusions To our list of eight impending changes and new priorities, municipal leaders could add other developments and trends visible on the horizon or unique to their region. The overall message here is not to predict these changes. It is rather to urge municipal governments to be realistic and clear sighted in their expectations. They must be anticipatory and proactive as they organize to deal with them. Municipal leaders must also have the courage to surmount traditional roadblocks to reform, both though vision and where appropriate, through institutional, political, cultural, and managerial reforms. His work focuses on the development of strate- gies to promote 21st century well-being in small town, rural, Indigenous, and remote Canada. His major project examines the potential contributions and negative impacts of emerging tech- remote communities. He can be reached at kennethcoates gmail. com or at 306-341-0545. Canada s thousands of small cities, towns, and villages have the right to feel beleaguered these days. The western industrial world is in the middle of a profound process of mass urbanization. Over half of the global population is now urban. Led by the soaring growth rates in major cities in the develop- ing world, that urban-rural balance is set to top even more decisively in the coming decades. For countless smaller centres across Canada, these are worrying times, to be sure. Economic growth has slowed or reversed in most communities, particu- larly in rural areas and resource-dependent towns. Out-migration continues apace. Industrial down- sizing has resulted in numerous factory closures, often stripping communities of one of their largest employers. In recent months, the announcement of a series of sawmill shutdowns in British Columbia sent shockwaves across the province. There are bright spots, to be sure, led by creative new-economy hotspots that are finding creative ways to respond to economic change. Many of these cen- tres a large number of which are booming are in the suburban fringe around major centres. Beauty spots are quite well, propelled by investments in recreational properties, lone eagle entrepreneurs attracted by quality of life opportunities, and the global magnetism of this country s many natural wonders. But the list of communities at risk is longer than the one of prominent success stories. Canadians should not underestimate the nature of the transition that is underway. Most if not all of national job growth has been concentrated in a small number of major cities. Major infrastructure investments are centred in the cities, where there is also formidable home and retail construction activ- ity. Immigrants gather in the larger centres, with few venturing out to small communities and many of those that do subsequently re-migrate to other cit- ies. The nation s research-intensive universities and polytechnics compete with the best in the world. Post-secondary institutions in the smaller communi- ties, however, struggle to attract enough students. Shift toward the City-State Economy We are on the cusp, nationally and globally, of a permanent shift toward a city-state economy, in which metropolitan areas extend their domination over their regions and, equally important, establish an international presence with fewer and fewer ties to the surrounding lands. Toronto does not depend as much as in the past on the vitality of Owen Sound or North Bay. Similarly, Vancouver s robust, outward-looking economy is increasingly disconnected economically and psychologically from the financial well-being of Kamloops, Prince George, or Cranbrook. City states look globally for competitors and opportunities. This is not a uniquely Canadian phenomenon and can be seen playing out in nations around the world. Japan s smaller centres are in free-fall; there are hundreds of thousands of abandoned homes in rural areas. Much the same is also true across Europe. Rural anger is spreading about precipitous economic decline, the flight of young people to City states and the future of Canada s smaller communities SEPTEMBER 2019 MUNICIPAL WORLD 11 cjdleng.com MUNICIPAL ENGINEERING CJDL has extensive reconstruction projects. We strive to ensure long term planning objectives are considered with replacement and upgrades to all servicing. the cities, and the absence of effective government policies to offset the dif- ficult transition, repeating a political process that gained currency in the U.S. in recent years. Mega-cities have become the norm, in countries rich and poor, from Tokyo and London to Nairobi and Mumbai. It is not as though the city states are uniformly overjoyed by the effects of this transformation. The list of problems is substantial: soaring housing prices in the major centres, intense competition for tradespeople in the construction fields, rental home shortages, ever-larger traffic jams, policing challenges, infrastructure shortfalls, and the myriad issues related to massive, globally driven economic transformation. Rapid growth is as dif- ficult to manage as precipitous economic dislocation. But governments, including at the federal and provincial levels in Canada, have been much more respon- sive to the needs of the cities than the slow and seemingly inexorable decline of smaller towns. Forces this large are difficult to over- come. Capital is flowing into the cities along with hundreds of thousands of people. Single-industry towns compete with dozens of others across the country for government attention. Technological transitions are seemingly accelerating rural and small-town decline at the same time that they are driving up growth and employment in urban-centre, high tech-based cities. Let us not expect a rapid and definitive solution to what has become a complex and accelerating challenge. Small Communities Face Numerous Challenges It is vital to recognize, further, that the primary challenge exists at the local level and not with provincial and federal governments. Identifying a competitive and sustainable strategy for economic development requires much more than a successful grant application, creative tax arrangements, or a new policy initia- tive. Smaller communities that make the transition to the world of a city-state economy focus on local transformation more than the recruitment of a single new factory. The transformation can take many forms, but comprehensive community engagement is essential. People have to recognize the real challenges facing their community and have to buy into the idea of a comprehensive rethinking of future possibilities. Young people have to be convinced that they are part of the transformation and that they have a clear role in the decades ahead. Chambers of commerce and key community lead- ers must put their weight, their time, and their resources behind a sustained effort to position the local area for a competitive future. Finally, communities have to take region-wide approaches to economic planning, recognizing that no single village, town, or small city has the resources to capture the opportunities that lie in the offing. What can small cities do next? For now, attention in Canada s smaller cities, towns, and villages could focus on three things: the development of a nationwide awareness of the challenges being experienced by non-metropolitan areas (and making sure that this becomes a major issue in the 2019 federal elec- tion), identifying and celebrating best local practices in responding to the formidable economic forces at play, and pressing for concerted national strategies for the revitalization and preservation of the small town fabric of Canada. We need to identify the problem, show that many smaller communities have strategies for competing globally, and work together to create a comprehensive Canadian plan to ensure that this coun- try does not continue along the path toward a truly city-state economy. This is the primary challenge of our time for rural areas, towns, and small cities. Assess the ways in which societal attitudes present obstacles to the expression of sexuality among people with disabilities. 2. Identify and discuss common myths and misperceptions regarding the sexuality of people with disabilities. 3. Describe the roles health professionals can play in promoting the sexual health of people with disabilities. Topic Overview Millions of Canadians live with either physical or developmental disabilities that impact on sexuality. Nearly all education and health professionals will be working, in one capacity or another, with clients with disabilities. Although there is a persistent and damaging myth that people with disabilities are somehow less sexual than other people, research has shown that, for example, most children with disabilities will experience sexual activity by early adulthood . Despite this, few youth with disabilities receive sexual health education suited to their needs (Holland-Hall and Quint, 2017; Schmidt, Brown, and Darragh, 2020). Thus, it is important for us to have a basic understanding of the ways that disabilities impact on sexuality and to provide the necessary health care and education to equip people with disabilities with the relevant information, motivation, and skills to overcome the obstacles to healthy sexual expression that they often face. Terminology: Identity-First and Person-First Language People with disability often have a strong preference for either person-first language (e.g., person with a disability) or identity-first language (e.g., disabled person). Person-first language became popular as a way to put the person first and not define a person by a disability they have. The use of person-first language was meant to promote the equality and dignity of people with disabilities. The American Psychological Association recommends the use of person- first language for writing about disability so first-person language will be used in this module. However, it is important to recognize and respect that some people in the disability community have advocated for the use of identity-first language (i.e. disabled Person) which recognizes that a disability can be central to a person's identity. Existing literature and first-person narratives suggest that identity-first language (i.e., disabled youth), which views disability as a core aspect is sometimes preferred among those with lived experience . What is most important, is that non-disabled people be led by, respect, and affirm each individual person with a disability's choice of preference for identity-first or person-first language. Definition of the term \"Physical Disability\" The term \"physical disability\" applies to physiological conditions affecting one or more of the following body systems: neurological, musculoskeletal, special sense organs, respiratory (including speech organs), cardiovascular, reproductive, digestive, genito-urinary, blood and lymphatic, skin, and endocrine. Examples of physical disabilities that can affect sexuality include Multiple Sclerosis, Cerebral Palsy, Spinal Cord Injuries, and Sensory Disabilities. Definition of the term \"Developmental Disability\" The term \"developmental disability\" commonly applies to conditions that first occur before the age of 18 and affect a person's ability to learn and apply conceptual, social, and practical skills in everyday life. Autism spectrum disorder is an example of a developmental disability. Note that disabilities that impair cognitive function can also begin in adulthood (Sometimes referred to as \"intellectual disabilities\"). Key Point Million's of Canadians live with either physical or developmental disabilities. It is therefore important for health professionals to have an understanding of the inter-connection of disability and sexuality and to provide education to enable people with disabilities to maximize their sexual health and well-being. On a broader level, an examination of the topic of sexuality and disability provides a unique opportunity to deepen our understanding of both the social context of human sexuality and the nature of sexuality itself. As we have seen in this course, we all carry with us a set of assumptions about what constitutes \"normal\", \"healthy\" or \"natural\" sexuality. The traditional sexual script and media representations of sexuality and sexual behaviour have given us a very narrow conception of \"normal\". People with disabilities sometimes do not fit damaging societal standards of sexual attractiveness and beauty. People with disabilities, then, are often forced to adapt their sexual expression to their circumstances in ways that fundamentally challenge our assumptions about \"normal\" sexuality and sexual response. Ableism and Sexual Health \"Traditional ideas regarding disability and sexuality are often profoundly sexist, ableist, and heteronormative. Moreover, commonly held ideas regarding disability are medicalized or ableist understandings that view disability as an unfortunate, individual, biomedical problem that requires treatment or rehabilitation\" . Ableism is a way of thinking based on negative beliefs (e.g., false assumptions and stereotypes) and feelings (e.g., discomfort, negative attitudes) about people with disabilities. Impact of ableism on the sexual health and well-being of disabled youth False assumptions being made about the ability and desire of disabled youth to date, be in relationships, have sexual experiences, and become parents; Not having discussions about sexuality and sexual health with disabled youth because disabled youth are believed to not have the capacity to make their own decisions about their sexuality; The sexual health needs and experiences of disabled youth not being included in sexual health education programs; Sexual and reproductive health services not being offered to disabled youth because they are believed to be irrelevant to them; and Not respecting the right of disabled youth to autonomously make decisions about their own sexual health and well-being. SIECCAN . If you are interested in understanding the sexual health education promotion needs of youth with disabilities see The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Promotion with Disabled Youth and Sexual Health Promotion: A Service Provider Guide for Working with Disabled Youth Key Point The topic of sexuality and disability demonstrates the variability in \"normal\" sexual response expression. As both Campbell and Di Giulio make clear, perhaps the most important lesson to be learned from an examination of this topic is that often the greatest obstacle to healthy sexual expression for a person with a disability is not the disability itself but rather the attitudes towards the sexuality of people with disabilities held by family, caregivers, and society in general. As we have seen throughout this course, sexuality represents a fundamental component of who we are as human beings and this is equally the case for people with physical or developmental disabilities. However, among the common misperceptions about people with disabilities is that they are either asexual, without sexual desires or sexual response or, in the case of people with developmental disabilities, that they are incapable of responsible sexual behaviour or healthy relationships. In sum, people with disabilities not only have to overcome the limitations that a disability may place upon their sexual expression, they must do so in a social environment that discourages or ignores their sexuality. If you are interested in understanding the sexual health education promotion needs of Autistic youth see The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Promotion with Autistic Youth and Sexual Health Promotion: A Service Provider Guide for Working with Autistic Youth Key Point The person with a disability faces the challenge of living in a society that holds many myths and misperceptions about sexuality and disability. Sexuality and Disability: Myths and Misperceptions As educators working towards positive social change, it is important that we seek to challenge and disrupt destructive and unjust ableist myths and misperceptions related to sexuality and disability. Unfortunately, the myths and misperceptions related to sexuality and disability listed below are still evident in our society. Physical Disability People with physical disabilities do not have the same levels of sexual desire and feelings as people without disabilities. People with physical disabilities have given up the sexual part of themselves. People with disabilities such as spinal cord injury are not capable of pleasurable and satisfying sexual experiences. Youth and adults with physical disabilities are not sexually active. Youth and adults with physical disabilities do not want or need sexuality education. Developmental Disability People with developmental disabilities are child-like in their sexual desires and behaviour. People with developmental disabilities should be discouraged from being sexual because they cannot control their sexual impulses. Youth with developmental disabilities should not receive sexuality education because it will lead to inappropriate behaviour. People with developmental disabilities are incapable of healthy sexual and romantic relationships. Contrary to prevailing myths and misconceptions, many disabled youth are interested in and or are engaging in sexual activity. In a survey of youth (ages 16 24) in Canada, about two-thirds of youth with disabilities reported having been sexually active: 65 had touched a partner s genitals for pleasure; 59 have had penis-vagina sex; 42 have had penis-anal sex, and 58 have had oral sex . The sexual activity of people with disabilities can look and feel different than for non-disabled people and these ways of engaging in sexual activity have traditionally been excluded from dominant sexual scripts . When people with disabilities internalize these dominant sexual scripts that do not account for or align with their needs and wants, sexual experiences can be unfulfilling. Sexuality and Disability: The Self-Fulfilling Prophecy The acceptance of these myths and misconceptions can lead to a self-fulfilling prophecy in which people with disabilities either internalize negative societal attitudes towards sexuality and disability or they live in environments in which healthy sexual expression is impossible. In other words, if society sends the consistent message that people with disabilities are not fully sexual or are incapable of satisfying, healthy sexual relationships, people with disabilities themselves may come to accept and be influenced by these destructive messages. Key Point Negative societal messages about sexuality and disability can lead to a self-fulfilling prophecy in which people with disabilities internalize these messages and retreat from intimacy and sexuality. For example, while physical disabilities, such as spinal cord injury, may place limits on sexual behaviour and response, feelings of sexual inadequacy and poor self-esteem are, in many respects, rooted in the acceptance of false societal attitudes and beliefs suggesting that people who use wheel chairs are less sexual. As a person in a wheel chair absorbs, and perhaps accepts, these negative messages, they may become sufficiently discouraged about the prospect of leading a satisfying sexual life that sexual feelings are avoided. When self-esteem falls and a negative body image develops, sexual desires and feelings may decline. This is particularly unfortunate given that restoring a sense of sexuality and positive feelings about being sexual can play an important role in helping people recovering from severe injury or illness rebuild a sense of well-being and quality of life. Prevailing attitudes towards, and the treatment of, people with developmental disabilities provides an even more dramatic example of this self-fulfilling prophecy. Often, people with developmental disabilities live in environments in which they are never alone or have the privacy for sexual expression. As a result, with no opportunity for privacy, any sexual behaviour or expression will occur in an inappropriate context. For example, a couple may have sex in a park because there is no opportunity to be alone in the group home where they live and sexual interaction is not allowed in the home. If they are caught, their behaviour will be seen as evidence that they are not capable of responsible sexual behaviour rather than as a possible indication that their need for intimacy and a lack of privacy led them to the park. Hingsburger and Tough give the example of an assessment test that was used with youth with developmental disabilities to illustrate how they are sometimes pushed to behave in ways that seem inappropriate but are in fact quite logical. The test asks the individual with a disability to Point to where it is OK to have sex. The individual is then shown a series of four pictures: a workplace, a park, a bedroom, and a bathroom. The correct answer, the bedroom, seems obvious. A person answering by pointing to any picture, other than that of the bedroom, is marked incorrect. Yet this question shows the naivete of many who do not understand real-world conditions for people with disabilities. Many people with disabilities point to the park. When it is suggested that the bedroom is the right answer, the individual often looks incredulously at the instructor. The bedroom, one person responded, we can t have sex in our bedrooms, we d get killed! For this man and his girlfriend, the park was where they went to engage in intimate behavior. It was private (sort of), safe (kind of), and away from eyes of staff and others who might punish . As Di Guilio points out, developmentally disabled youth can often benefit from enhanced social skills training as part of the sexuality education that they require. Yet, as we have seen, many youth with developmental disabilities do not receive appropriate sexuality education but instead absorb sex negative messages designed to prevent sexual expression. The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education offer the following guidance with respect to short and long-term care facilities serving youth, adults, and seniors with disabilities: Children, youth, adults, and seniors with developmental, intellectual, and physical disabilities, as well as people with chronic illnesses, often lack access to sexual health education relevant to their specific needs. Every person has individual needs, strengths, and challenges. Comprehensive sexual health education should be adapted to clients' specific learning needs. Personnel in these facilities can be provided with opportunities for in-service and continuing education to increase their capacity to provide comprehensive sexual health education. Staff should be trained to connect individuals to appropriate services . Toward Healthy Sexuality Among People with Disabilities: The Role of the Health Professional Myths and misperceptions surrounding the sexuality of people with disabilities are wide spread and in many cases health professionals are not immune to these biases. To the extent that health professionals assume that sexual desires and expression are either absent or inappropriate for people with disabilities, they may perpetuate and increase the barriers to healthy sexuality that the disabled already face. Key Point Many health professionals hold common myths and misperceptions about sexuality and disability and are uncomfortable addressing sexuality issues in their work. Comprehensive health care for people with disabilities should include the identification and removal of barriers to sexual health and the provision of sexual health education and services that will enable youth and adults with disabilities to enhance their sexual health and avoid sexual health problems. Listed below are five basic steps that health professionals can take to promote the sexual health of people with disabilities. Acknowledge and Respect Acknowledge and respect the right of people with disabilities to sexual health education and clinical services: On a general level, many health professionals are uncomfortable addressing sexuality issues with patients clients and this discomfort may be particularly acute with respect to people with disabilities. This discomfort on the part of the health professional can translate into the avoidance or neglect of the sexual health education and services needs of people with disabilities. Thus, an important first step for the health professional is to recognize, acknowledge, and respect the right of all people, including people with physical and developmentally disabilities, to relevant and comprehensive sexual health education and services. Knowledge and Skills Develop the knowledge and skills to provide brief education and therapy: As noted above, there are a wide range of physical and developmental disabilities, each of which have specific and distinct implications for sexuality. Thus, health professionals can educate themselves about the relevant sexual health issues as they apply to the specific disabilities of their patients clients. Once a health professional is equipped with knowledge about the sexuality implications of a specific disability, they can become more confident and proactive in inquiring if their patients clients have sexual concerns. For example, a health professional who frequently works with patients who have recently acquired a disability (e.g., multiple sclerosis, spinal cord injury) or chronic illness (e.g., heart disease, cancer) can significantly increase the quality of care if they are equipped to ask and answer questions about sexuality during a routine assessment. Because sexuality is a sensitive or embarrassing topic for many people, many patients clients will not ask about the sexuality implications of their disability or medical condition unless they are invited to do so by their health care provider. When patients do have concerns related to sexuality, they will greatly appreciate being given the opportunity and encouragement to ask questions. Beginning a Dialogue About Sexuality with Youth with Disabilities It is important that we all take care of our sexual health. Many young people have questions about relationships, dating, and sex. Is it okay if I ask you a couple of questions about some of these things? Visit the Disability and Sexuality Resource Hub for examples of conversations guides and other resources that can be helpful to service providers: Di Giulio summarizes the PLISSIT model (permission, limited information, specific suggestions, intensive therapy) for providing basic sexual health education and therapy to people with disabilities. Deliver Education Where necessary, develop and deliver relevant sexual health education programs: Institutions (e.g., hospitals, long-term care facilities) and community service agencies that address the needs of people with disabilities can create and implement sexual health education programs tailored to the specific needs of their patients clients. The elicitation, intervention, evaluation process for applying the information, motivation, behavioural skills model to sexual health education recommended by the Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health can be used to develop effective programming for people with disabilities (We will looking more closely at using these concepts to provide effective sexual health education programs in the next module). Policy Development Promote organizational policy development: Many institutions and agencies serving people with disabilities do not have specific policies regarding sexuality. Policies related to sexuality may be particularly important for long-term care facilities or for agencies serving clients with highly restricted mobility. Without such policies, important sexuality issues may be addressed inconsistently, ineffectively, or not at all. The development of policies regarding sexuality not only contributes to greater consistency in how sexuality issues are addressed, they provide a basis upon which institutional agency administration, staff, clients, and client families can communicate and consult on issues related to sexuality, sexuality education, and sexual health services. Typically, policies regarding sexuality begin with statements of principle affirming the rights of people with disabilities to sexual expression and access to sexuality information. Applying the Principles of Disability Justice to Sexual Health Promotion with Youth with Disabilities Disability justice is a cross-disability framework that values individual differences and works to address the collective experience of disadvantage of marginalized groups to promote a society where all bodies and minds are valued. A disability justice framework can be applied to the sexual health promotion of youth with disabilities. Utilizing a disability justice approach, sexual health promotion with youth with disabilities should, Consider intersectionality (i.e., understanding how multiple social identities, such as race, gender identity, and sexual orientation, intersect to create unique experiences of advantage disadvantage for diverse youth with disabilities) Promote the leadership of those who are most impacted (i.e., valuing and prioritizing the perspectives of those who have been the most disadvantaged by societal systems and who understand the systems best through their lived experiences) Work to challenge ableist social norms, which do not account for the needs and experiences of people people with disabilities Commit to cross-movement organizing (i.e., working with other disadvantaged groups to address shared experiences of systemic disadvantages that contribute to sexual health inequities) Recognize wholeness (i.e., recognizing the many dimensions of a person, such as their experiences, strengths, and the many aspects of their identity, as well as the inherent worth of all individuals) Encourage each individual to honour their bodies, needs, and abilities Commit to cross-disability solidarity (i.e., working to address the collective experience of disadvantage that contribute to the sexual health inequities experienced by all members of the disability community) Promote interdependence (i.e., recognizing the value of reciprocal relationships and community support) Promote collective access (i.e., working to create spaces and systems where everyone s accessibility needs are considered and addressed) Leave no one behind (i.e., working to ensure that sexual health promotion accounts for and addresses the needs of all people with disabilities) Adapted from SIECCAN. . Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Promotion with Disabled Youth. Toronto, ON. Sex Information and Education Council of Canada . Staff and Family Educate staff and family members: In most cases, the successful implementation of an institutional agency policy regarding sexuality requires the provision of information sessions for clients, client family members (e.g., parents) and training seminars for staff. First and foremost, clients need to be included, where possible, in the policy development process and made aware of available education and clinic services. Family members should be informed of the policy and given the opportunity to attend information education sessions. Finally, it should be noted that most health professionals do not receive specific training, as part of their education, on providing sexual health education generally and even fewer receive training on providing sexuality education and services to people with disabilities. Staff members who have acquired knowledge and skills in this area can play an indispensable role in advocating for, and delivering, staff training sessions on sexuality and disability. Andrews, E. Understand the roles of Information, Motivation, and Behavioural Skills in changing sexual health related behaviour. 2. Topic Overview The primary objectives of this course have been to encourage a critical understanding of the social context of human sexuality and to introduce key theories and concepts relevant to the study of human sexuality. We have also paid particular attention to how these concepts can be applied to the work of health professionals addressing sexuality issues in their work. As we have examined the developmental tasks of each phase of the life-cycle and topics such as gender, sexual orientation, and disability, it has become evident that sexuality and sexual health are important components of individual and community well-being. One of the outcomes of the gradual transition from a Restrictive to a Permissive sexual ideology in Western culture is that it is increasingly accepted that in order to comprehensively and holistically meet the needs of their constituents, health professionals (e.g., nurses, rehabilitation counsellors, social workers, and educators) must be willing and able to address issues of sexuality. If we are to utilize our knowledge and expertise in human sexuality to the benefit of our students, clients and communities, it is crucial to incorporate effective, well-tested approaches to sexual health promotion in our work. The aim of this final module is to provide you with a basic general approach to developing and implementing effective sexual health promotion activities. Please Note To effectively promote sexual health, health professionals will benefit from using well-tested, behaviourally focused approaches to sexual health enhancement and problem prevention. We are at a unique juncture in history and have a rare opportunity to develop global, national, and community strategies to promote sexual health for the new century. This opportunity has been created by the fact that the world is experiencing a new sexual revolution and a public health imperative. Much like the sexual revolution of the 1960s and 1970s, it is a revolution fuelled by incredible scientific advances, as well as a dramatic social and economic change We also face a myriad of sexual health problems, which is creating an enormous burden on societies. These two factors are putting pressure on health ministries to develop comprehensive approaches to sexual health promotion . The Information, Motivation, Behavioural Skills Model for Sexual and Reproductive Health Promotion In Module 8, Part 2, we saw how the information, motivation, behavioural skills theoretical model can be applied to adolescent sexuality education. Although it was initially developed and tested as a theoretical model to guide interventions targeting HIV AIDS sexual risk reduction, the IMB model can be used to guide a wide range of sexual health promotion and education activities . Whether we are creating and implementing programs to promote sexual health with individuals, groups, or communities, the IMB model provides a basic and well-tested conceptual framework for ensuring that our interventions are effective in reaching their objectives. First and foremost, sexual and reproductive health behaviors, whether they involve sexual function promotion, contraceptive utilization, STD HIV prevention, reproductive cancer screening, or sexual adaptations to aging, illness, or disability, represent sexualized behavioral events. Each of these sexual and reproductive health behaviors has acquired sexual meaning as a result of social ascription, individual learning, and the sexual and reproductive biology and physiology involved in all of the acts and adjustments in question. As sexualized acts, sexual and reproductive behaviors are all influenced by a common set of determinants of sexual behavior, including information, motivation, and behavioral skills factors, and all are potentially modifiable via common intervention pathways focusing on changing these factors in efforts to promote sexual and reproductive health (Fisher and Fisher, 1998, p. s 39-40). The IMB Model for Sexual Health Promotion Acquisition of Information Individual must learn information that is relevant to the practice of sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Development of Motivation Individual must become personally motivated and or socially motivated to practice sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Development of Behavioural Skills Individual must learn the actual behavioural skills to perform sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Although the necessity of including relevant information, motivation, and behavioural skills in sexual health promotion programs seems obvious, very few existing programs actually contain all three elements. In many cases, sexual health education programs have been exclusively information-based and, in particular, have neglected to provide specific behavioural skills training. Key Point The degree to which an individual is able to enact sexual health promoting behaviour will be dependant to a significant extent on their level of relevant information, motivation, and behavioural skills. Case Example An IMB Model Intervention Approach to Assist Women with Sexual Function Difficulties Related to Chemotherapy Treatment for Breast Cancer. Research suggests that difficulty with sexual functioning is one of the most distressing problems for long-term survivors of breast cancer and that in many cases these problems are related to vaginal dryness resulting from chemotherapy treatment (e.g., see Broeckel, Thors, Jacobsen, Small, and Cox, 2002). Therefore, one of the objectives of a sexual health education intervention for this group might be to target information, motivation, and behaviour skills related to the use of vaginal lubrication prior to sexual activity. Information: (Must be relevant, complete, and easy to translate into behaviour) Examples of relevant information: Many women treated for breast cancer experience problems with sexual function. These problems are often associated with vaginal dryness. Methods to relieve vaginal dryness, including the use of lubricants prior to sexual activity, can be used to improve sexual function among women treated for breast cancer. Lubricants (e.g. Astroglide) are available, without prescription, at the pharmacy in the Family Planning section where the condoms are displayed. Motivation: (Must help the recipient develop a positive attitude towards the behaviour by recognizing that she will benefit from engaging in the behaviour personal motivation and that there is some form of external support for engaging in the behaviour social motivation ). Examples of motivational messages: Restoring sexual function and enjoyment can play an important role in regaining an overall sense of well-being following treatment for breast cancer (personal motivation). Applying a lubricant prior to sexual activity can be an effective and easy to use method for helping to restore sexual function (personal motivation). Other women experiencing vaginal dryness report that using lubricants is very helpful and health professionals recommend their use for this purpose (social motivation). Behavioural Skills:(Must include the teaching of the specific behavioural skills needed to initiate and maintain the behaviour). Examples of behavioural skills training: Instruction on how to locate lubricant in the pharmacy. Instruction on the application of vaginal lubrication (encourage practice and experimentation). Discussion and decision-making on when in the sexual script lubricant will be applied. Practice and rehearsal of communication with partner about the use of lubricants (give explicit and clear examples of communication and or incorporate role-playing exercises where participants practice bringing up and discussing the use of lubricants with a partner). Applying the IMB Model: Elicitation, Intervention, Evaluation Effectively applying the IMB model to a sexual health promotion intervention involves a basic three-step process: elicitation, intervention, and evaluation. In the elicitation phase, program planners assess or measure the target audience s information, motivation, and behavioural skills related to a specific set of sexual health behaviours. This can be done through focus groups, assessment interviews with clients, or questionnaires. In other words, conducting elicitation research will allow program planners to identify which aspects of information, motivation, and behavioural skills the target audience is lacking. Instead of creating programs based on our own assumptions or best guesses as to what the target audience needs, conducting elicitation research helps to ensure that an intervention will be relevant and have the desired impact. In the second step , the program is created and implemented based on the information, motivation, behavioural skills deficits weaknesses of the target audience. In the third step, the program is periodically assessed (again using focus groups, interviews, questionnaires, ect) to ensure that the program is successfully impacting on the information, motivation, and behavioural skills of the target audience. This information can be used to refine and improve the intervention. Key Point The comprehensive, systematic application of an IMB-based sexual health promotion program involves a three-step process of elicitation research, intervention, and evaluation. This program of information motivation behavioural skills and elicitation intervention evaluation will give you a basic structural framework for the delivery of effective sexual health promotion activities. Your required reading for this module is a section of the Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education. This section, titled Key Components provides further description and discussion of the IMB model. The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education are a recommended resource support document for health professionals involved in the provision of sexual health education and promotion programs. Key Point The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education provide support and guidance for the development and implementation of effective sexual health education and promotion initiatives. Opportunities for Further Professional Development in the Area of Sexuality The field of sexology has advanced significantly in recent decades. Parallel to the increasing openness towards sexuality in Western culture, the importance of research on human sexuality and sexual health promotion has been increasingly recognized. In addition, as we have seen throughout this course, addressing sexuality and sexual health issues is also gradually becoming an accepted component of the practice of a wide variety of helping professions. However, despite these advancements, it is also important to be aware that sexology has not yet reached the status of a distinct academic discipline. For example, few universities have sexology departments. Indeed, at present, only one sexology department exists in Canada, located at the University of Quebec at Montreal (UQAM s sexology programs are given in the french language). Several universities in Canada offer programs that enable students to specialize in human sexuality (e.g., Graduate programs in the Department of Family Studies at the University of Guelph; Interdisciplinary Studies in Sexuality, Marriage and the Family at the University of Waterloo). For most students seeking to become specialists in sexuality research, education, counselling or therapy, the most advantagious route to so is to pursue graduate studies in an appropriate related academic program (e.g., nursing, clinical, counselling or educational psychology, medicine, social work, education, ect.) and, to the extent possible, focus one s research and study on sexuality within that program. Many graduate programs have faculty members with an interest in sexology and who teach courses related to sexuality. Obtaining additional credentials in one of these more traditional disciplines also provides a broader qualification than training limited to sexology. This is an important factor to consider in choosing a graduate program. In sum, if you want to become a sexuality educator, become an educator first, if you want to become a medical sexologist, become a physician or nurse first, if you want to become a sex therapist, become a therapist first. In other words, first and foremost, establish your credentials in your discipline as you pursue your focus on sexuality. Becoming a Sex Therapist Technically, there are no licensing bodies in Canada that legally certify a person as a sex therapist. Generally speaking, a well-qualified sex therapist has advanced graduate training in an appropriate discipline such as medicine, psychology, social work, nursing ect. as well as additional specific training in sex therapy. Specific training in sex therapy is available through organizations such as the American Association of Sex Educators, Counselors, and Therapists and the Association of Sex Therapy in Ontario (ASTO: A Final Word This course has been ambitious in its scope and objectives. You have been asked to not only develop a critical understanding of the social context of human sexuality. You have also been asked to acquire a wide-ranging knowledge of sexuality issues and concepts that are crucial to the effective promotion of sexual health. You have taken an important first step in developing the knowledge and skills to make a meaningful contribution to the sexual health of those you are or will be working with. Enhancing the ability of individuals, couples, families, and communities to achieve healthy sexuality, a fundamental component of human well-being, is something to be proud of.
